NEWSLETTER · 29 Oct 2026, 22:50

The most expensive election in history

Global power is in flux. Your daily guide to what comes next.

By JACK BLANCHARD

Send tips here | View in your browser

EVERY POWER CENTER. ONE NEW APP: The new POLITICO app has arrived, bringing the full breadth of our journalism and professional intelligence together for the first time. As the U.S. midterms approach and consequential elections loom around the world, follow how decisions in one country shape politics and policy in another. Get personalized coverage from across our geographies, plus Playbooks, podcasts, live reporting and the best of our POLITICO Pro intelligence. Download here.

Welcome to POLITICO Forecast. This is Jack Blanchard, sending this newsletter surrounded by two unfathomably excited children in costume, plotting their biggest weekend of the year. Halloween in this part of the world really is something else. Wish us luck!

5 THINGS THAT MATTERED TODAY

— A record $500 billion was wiped off Google owner Alphabet’s value after “AI mode” searches hit its core advertising business.

— European Central Bank chief Christine Lagarde announced she will step down in Feb. 2027.

— Israeli opposition leader Gadi Eisenkot accused PM Benjamin Netanyahu of an “organised campaign” to “delegitimize the results” of this week’s election.

— European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen restated her wish for Canada to become an “associate member” of the EU, despite concern in European capitals.

— U.S. intelligence agencies fear Nvidia boss Jensen Huang has been the target of a Chinese spy plot, the NYT reported.

THE NEXT NEWS CYCLE

President Donald Trump attends a campaign rally on Oct. 26, 2026 in Mississippi. | Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images

IT’S STILL THE ECONOMY, STUPID: If you want one number to help you understand the U.S. midterm elections, try this — $801 million.

That’s the total spent on TV adverts in this year’s top races on just a single issue — affordability, A.K.A. the sky-high cost of living in America.

Repeatedly derided by Donald Trump as a “hoax,” affordability has long topped almost every opinion poll as the issue Americans care about most. That’s bad news for a president who came to power last year vowing to bring prices down, then watched as inflation rose and gas prices soared under his watch.

Despite Trump’s best efforts, this has become the affordability election. As POLITICO’s Paroma Soni and Aaron Pellish report in today’s Morning Score newsletter, the Democrats alone have spent $401 million on affordability ads this year. Republicans have attempted a rearguard action, spending $311 million — much of it focused on their 2025 tax cuts.

For Forecast readers outside of America, these numbers will look astronomical.

Most other Western democracies impose strict campaign spending limits, at least during election periods. In the 2024 U.K. general election campaign, the total spent by all political parties was £94.5 million ($121 million). In the 2022 French presidential election campaign, the total spent was €83.5m ($90 million).

But even for our American readers — well used to the cost of doing politics over here — we are entering uncharted territory.

The tracking service AdImpact announced this week that total nationwide political ad spending during this U.S. midterm election cycle — meaning the calendar years 2025 and 2026 combined — has now hit more than $11.2 billion. That’s more than the total spent in the 2024 presidential campaign cycle, previously the most expensive election in history.

“This is by far the most expensive midterm election ever,” Aaron tells Forecast, “and as of Tuesday, it is the most expensive election in U.S. history.”

The outsized role ad money plays in U.S. politics currently favors Republicans, who have far more cash to throw around than their Democratic opponents. GOP donor money is still pouring into battleground states like Texas, where Republicans must cling on if they’re to keep control of the U.S. Senate. If Republicans have a better night than expected on Tuesday, their ad spend will be one big reason why.

But Dems say spending matters only to a point — and that nothing trumps political advertising like the cold, hard facts staring you in the face. And for most Americans, affordability is an issue measured not by TV ads but in sticker shock at the supermarket, or in the price they pay at the pump when filling up their vehicles.

It’s certainly striking how closely the surge in U.S. gas prices since the summer tracks with a surge in support for Democrats over the same period. This Reuters/Ipsos tracker poll shows that in late July, when gas prices were at their lowest since the war in Iran began, Dems held only a two-point polling advantage over the Republicans. Three months later, as gas prices have steadily shot up again, Dems have extended their lead to a whopping 13 points.

None of that bodes well for Trump and the GOP, five days out from Election Day. And the current average price of gas in the U.S. is now $4.22 a gallon, which means — barring some weekend miracle — that pump prices will be more expensive on Nov. 3 than on any election day on record.

When the story of the 2026 midterms is written, that number might just prove to be the most important one of all.

FUTURE INTELLIGENCE

CHANGING OF LAGARDE: The starting gun just sounded in the race for one of the biggest jobs in global finance.

And the battle to succeed Christine Lagarde as head of the European Central Bank is already a red-hot political issue, with cries of a political stitch-up among Europe’s far right.

A bandaged Lagarde — still recovering from a skin cancer operation — announced today that she will step down from her role as ECB chair in February, eight months earlier than planned. Lagarde, a former French finance minister, said she wants “freedom of expression” to speak out during the upcoming French election.

But her timetable for departure has drawn accusations of unfair maneuvering from the French far right, who hope to win the presidency — and thus secure a key role in deciding Lagarde’s successor — in May.

“As our resident ECB expert Johanna Treeck wrote — this ‘Le Pen-proofs’ the ECB,” POLITICO’s EU financial services editor Fiona Maxwell tells Forecast. “The timing means outgoing French President Emmanuel Macron will have a say in choosing Lagarde’s successor ahead of a possible presidential win for far-right leader Marine Le Pen, who could take a radically different view on the ECB.

“It’s particularly important for France at the moment,” Fiona notes, “given its debt troubles and the wobbly markets.”

The new ECB head will now be agreed by eurozone leaders before the end of the year, German Chancellor Friedrich Merz said today. The shortened timetable mirrors efforts by Europe’s leaders to agree a new seven-year EU budget by December — thus preventing Le Pen from blowing up the financial talks next year.

There are two frontrunners for the ECB job, Fiona says — neither of whom hail from the eurozone’s most powerful nations. France has already held the role twice, while Germany already has two major posts at the European Commission and the ECB’s supervisory arm.

Instead, leaders are weighing the merits of two figures from the eurozone’s central banking circuit — Spain’s Pablo Hernández de Cos, an economist with a reputation for quiet authority, and Dutchman Klaas Knot, a former interest rate hawk who spent several years on the ECB’s Governing Council.

Neither would be seen as a radical departure for the ECB — much to Marine Le Pen’s undoubted chagrin.

QUOTE OF THE DAY

“An ocean separates us, but the distance between us has never felt so small.”

— European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen hails Canada’s blossoming relationship with the EU.

HARD POWER: ICED OUT

The Castner Glacier pictured on May 5, 2023 near Paxson, Alaska. | Mario Tama/Getty Images

If next week’s midterm elections leave the U.S. Senate on a knife-edge, get ready to hear plenty more about Alaska.

As one of several Republican-held Senate seats Democrats are aiming to flip in Tuesday’s elections, the Last Frontierwas already expected to play a crucial role in deciding which party controls the upper chamber.

Suddenly, that battle looks like it may play out in the courts. The NYT reported this week that Democratic operatives in Alaska have been involved in a secret scheme to draw spoiler candidates into the race.

This nefarious practice isn’t exactly unheard of in American politics. But Democrats, including their Senate candidate Mary Peltola, have long denied any involvementin an effort to siphon votes from Republican Sen. Dan S. Sullivan by recruiting a second candidate of almost the same name, Dan J. Sullivan, onto the ballot.

While it’s unclear anything illegal has occurred, Republicans spy an opportunity.

The Department of Justice had already filed suit last week to block state officials from certifying the results, if the incumbent Republican senator loses. U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche then doubled down after the ballot-stacking allegations came out, threatening further action.

President Trump demanded Peltola be removed from the ballot altogether. And Alaska’s Republican governor has now ordered an investigation into her campaign.

“It’s hard to believe [Peltola] could be allowed to run,” Trump told the press today. “You have Dan Sullivan, who is a great senator. You have another guy named Dan Sullivan, who never even, I think, voted before. He didn’t campaign. He was just put there as a fraud. … It’s called voter fraud.”

Watch for plenty more fallout. Alaska is the last state in the nation to close its polls on Election Day, and it was already expected to be slow in reporting its election results. Now there are even more signs pointing to a muddied or delayed result — and perhaps a long, bitter, drawn-out legal battle. If control of the Senate hangs in the balance, that could prove critical.

Playbook’s own Irie Sentner spent a week chatting with voters in Alaska earlier this month, before the ballot-stacking scandal broke. Even then, he found the MAGA faithful were suspicious of the second Sullivan’s intentions.

But for the most part, Trump voters didn’t seem too concerned about the Sullivan threat. Or, as one voter told Irie: “If people are that stupid, then we deserve it.” Perhaps the mood has grown a little more sour now.

SOFT POWER: THE KING’S SPEECH

A Commonwealth Heads of Government meeting gets underway in the Caribbean island of Antigua this weekend — but it won’t be all beach-hopping and boat cruises for the guest of honor.

King Charles III has been enjoying a week of traditional Caribbean hospitality ahead of the biennial summit. But as ever these days, when a U.K. leader visits this part of the world, Britain’s past involvement in the slave trade has returned to the fore.

Prior to the trip, Buckingham Palace had said the King would not shy away from Britain’s “colonial legacy.” In a speech in the Bahamas this week, Charles spoke of a national culture “born out of darkness” and said it was “only right” to acknowledge historic injustices.

But Caribbean leaders want far more. First — a formal apology from Britain’s head of state for past wrongs. And more tangibly, financial reparations for the centuries of mass kidnapping, exploitation and murder. U.K. leaders continue to resist either step, insisting current generations are not responsible for centuries-old events.

Campaigners thus accused Charles of “hollow platitudes.” “The royal family and the British government were responsible for the ‘darkness’,” the Bahamas National Reparations Committee said in a statement. “His comments fall short of what justice requires.”

The stand-off certainly made for awkward viewing as a besuited Charles, 77, took part in a colorful street parade in Nassau this week, surrounded by protesters waving placards demanding justice. See you on Sunday.

WEEKEND AGENDA

— Second day of EU-Canada summit in Montreal on Friday.

— G20 foreign ministers convene in the U.S. state of Georgia on Friday and Saturday, with Russian envoy Sergey Lavrov expected to attend.

— President Trump campaigns in Florida on Friday and in Iowa on Sunday ahead of next week’s U.S. midterm elections.

— Oil giants ExxonMobil and Chevron announce Q3 profits on Friday.

— The Bank of Japan announces its latest interest rate decision on Friday.

— Commonwealth Heads of Government meeting kicks off on Sunday.

The Latest on Southeast Asia: Thailand-Cambodia Disputes Ongoing, One Year After Peace Accords

Cambodia and Thailand continue to seek a resolution to border disputes on land and sea a year after the October 26, 2025 signing of what is unofficially called the Kuala Lumpur Peace Accord.

Thailand-Cambodia Disputes Ongoing, One Year After Peace Accords

Cambodia and Thailand continue to seek a resolution to border disputes on land and sea a year after the October 26, 2025 signing

https://pardot.csis.org/e/906722/news-world-40072757/3vdkq5/4482935332/h/XOPQkwGn4XpppNgAblo8th5GwiUFDU2txBitJqDrjgE

of what is unofficially called the Kuala Lumpur Peace Accord. That joint statement was signed by prime ministers Anutin Charnvirakul of Thailand and Hun Manet of Cambodia and witnessed by President Donald Trump and Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim of Malaysia on the sidelines of last year’s ASEAN Summit. The agreement strengthened a late-July 2025 ceasefire that had halted days of fighting along the Thailand-Cambodia border. The Kuala Lumpur agreement broke down in early December 2025, but a second ceasefire was reached after three weeks of fighting and remains in place.

Military representatives from both countries met most recently on October 28 to discuss

https://pardot.csis.org/e/906722/mplementation--google-vignette/3vdkq8/4482935332/h/XOPQkwGn4XpppNgAblo8th5GwiUFDU2txBitJqDrjgE

the implementation of the December ceasefire

https://pardot.csis.org/e/906722/gory-detail-id-3243-iid-459719/3vdkqc/4482935332/h/XOPQkwGn4XpppNgAblo8th5GwiUFDU2txBitJqDrjgE

. Both sides emphasized the importance of operational coordination and trust-building in achieving a peaceful settlement of disagreements. But the ceasefire remains shaky, with Bangkok this week dismissing a Cambodian demand

https://pardot.csis.org/e/906722/ai-claims-over-border-temples-/3vdkqg/4482935332/h/XOPQkwGn4XpppNgAblo8th5GwiUFDU2txBitJqDrjgE

for the return of artifacts from two border temples, insisting they are on Thai territory. Cambodia also condemned Thai military operations near these heritage sites. The Royal Thai Army responded

https://pardot.csis.org/e/906722/news-general-40072693/3vdkqk/4482935332/h/XOPQkwGn4XpppNgAblo8th5GwiUFDU2txBitJqDrjgE

that its presence is necessary for mine clearance and security, while also claiming to have evidence that Cambodia used temple complexes for military purposes during last year’s fighting.

At the same time, Cambodian and Thai authorities are engaged in dialogue regarding

https://pardot.csis.org/e/906722/pute-over-us300-billion-seabed/3vdkqn/4482935332/h/XOPQkwGn4XpppNgAblo8th5GwiUFDU2txBitJqDrjgE

their overlapping maritime claims in the Gulf of Thailand. After Bangkok in May terminated

https://pardot.csis.org/e/906722/b34459c4d42a931d933b2df3c20aa8/3vdkqr/4482935332/h/XOPQkwGn4XpppNgAblo8th5GwiUFDU2txBitJqDrjgE

a 2001 memorandum of understanding on managing oil and gas resources in disputed waters, Phnom Penh initiated compulsory conciliation proceedings under the UN Convention on the Law of the Sea. This marks

https://pardot.csis.org/e/906722/ime-border-dispute-2026-09-15-/3vdkqv/4482935332/h/XOPQkwGn4XpppNgAblo8th5GwiUFDU2txBitJqDrjgE

just the second time that conciliation process has been used, the first being Timor-Leste’s successful case against Australia in 2016.

Delegations from the foreign ministries of Cambodia and Thailand met

https://pardot.csis.org/e/906722/iland-cambodia-standoff-571155/3vdkqy/4482935332/h/XOPQkwGn4XpppNgAblo8th5GwiUFDU2txBitJqDrjgE

in The Hague from October 14 to 18 for the round of Conciliation Commission discussions. Representatives agreed

https://pardot.csis.org/e/906722/pute-over-us300-billion-seabed/3vdkqn/4482935332/h/XOPQkwGn4XpppNgAblo8th5GwiUFDU2txBitJqDrjgE

to tone down rhetoric against each other and establish a hotline between Cambodian and Thai naval commanders to minimize misunderstandings and prevent conflict. This followed

https://pardot.csis.org/e/906722/ime-border-dispute-2026-09-15-/3vdkqv/4482935332/h/XOPQkwGn4XpppNgAblo8th5GwiUFDU2txBitJqDrjgE

their first conciliation meeting held in Singapore in September, during which both sides accused

https://pardot.csis.org/e/906722/ime-border-dispute-2026-09-15-/3vdkqv/4482935332/h/XOPQkwGn4XpppNgAblo8th5GwiUFDU2txBitJqDrjgE

the other of inflaming tensions amid strong nationalist sentiment and heated social media discourse.

NEWSLETTER · 29 Oct 2026, 21:09

Cogs of War Bi-Weekly Update

A Tale of Two Markets, a Responsibly Mined Missile, and Chip History on Repeat ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌

Good afternoon.

In this issue, our contributors explore the Pentagon’s dependence on markets and supply chains it doesn’t control. A former defense tech CEO describes the defense tech sector’s split into two markets, and warns that companies without production contracts may soon run out of time. A critical minerals consultant makes the case for using existing responsible-sourcing frameworks to track defense supply chains. A chip researcher discusses the history of chip shortages and export controls, and how that cycle is currently repeating itself in AI, and three energy researchers propose a strategic battery reserve for military drones. A former military technology leader and a tech executive argue that AI should allow for smaller military headquarters, as long as they keep enough experienced officers to check the machines’ work. And a current and a former special operations official drop by the studio to discuss with Jonathan how to effectively buy hundreds of thousands of drones, and the path ahead for Project Agincourt.

TOP OF THE AGENDA

Shock of rail tragedy gives way to sorrow and outrage in politically divided Spain

https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDQsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABqFjFiVqlJOzX3EJBHH_HbtyDSeFOKC3czWBJx_M3PR25S1jP3fbaKRk_PvyWctCHRt9hxf67YemBKMTfkqeCdhtJIfuGGbuk86drd6f At least 115 people have been killed in two big train crashes in Spain this year. Credit: EPA/Shutterstock

As Spain holds three days of mourning in the wake of its worst rail disaster since 2013, the "chaos and horror" of what happened has "given way to public disbelief and outrage" in "a highly politicised climate", write my colleagues Guy Hedgecoe at the scene in Urda, and Europe digital editor Paul Kirby. Another crash in January left 46 dead, and people in Spain are once again questioning "whether the network [is] fit for purpose", they write. And, they note, Wednesday's crash piles pressure on the embattled left-wing government of Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez, a month ahead of an election in which his socialist party is behind the conservatives in most opinion polls, in a campaign that now looks "increasingly likely" to be dominated by rail safety.

AT THE SCENE

Tokyo, Japan

Why bargain hunters are seeking out Japan's 'haunted homes'

https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDQsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABqFjFiV9z6CO8zx1WR78otgr8EMdxz85fMVwtHdgYNaMoj8mWXXDhaNdoFlxQZAeSN30kQFhP0GPFWk2nn_2jAIrZQ-CWP4ygY6FCv2U Ryua said she's 'a bit of a contrarian' and decided she 'wanted to become friends' with a 'ghost' in her flat.

While Japan has increasingly marked Halloween in recent years, many people take ghosts seriously all year round. The country has a word, "yuurei", which means a lingering, troubled spirit - believed to be the ghost of someone who died in violent or tragic circumstance. If they died at home, the belief is that their yuurei won't go away, and for house hunters who are not superstitious, it means there are bargains to be found.

Michael Fitzpatrick, business reporter

From the moment Ryua first walked into her rental apartment in Tokyo she believed it to be haunted. "When I visited the place for a viewing there was a man standing there, glaring at me," she says. "Since the estate agent couldn't see him, I realised he must be a ghost."

While someone more superstitious would probably have quickly left the flat, never to return, Ryua chose to move in. Four years later, she says she continues to experience paranormal activity on a daily basis and hears footsteps and the ghost talking. She was told that a man had killed himself in her apartment. It meant that she gets an almost 30% discount on her rent.

TOP OF THE AGENDA

Recordings released as Spain begins three days of mourning

https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDQsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABp1rK0k2SPlnoFofJE-hWAGlzy1EM8WB1jq4HNrNiP0Z2KP9f5y9UeN_N_NmQpGYybnaVa3R8rrjptq9Bs_uf4g4wUR5FvDqSZjNe1cv Iryo said its train had 147 people on board while the Renfe train was carrying 397 passengers. Credit: AFP via Getty Images

Three audio recordings between the control centre in Madrid and two train drivers shed more light on what happened minutes before the fatal crash. They appear to show that one high-speed train was given clearance to move even though another had stalled. The ensuing collision is one of the worst rail disasters in Europe's modern history. At least 69 people have died, while rescuers were working through the night to search for victims, with many of those killed still to be identified. Protesters gathered in Madrid overnight, reportedly calling for the resignation of the prime minister and the transport minister.

Joe Perticone - The Bulwark

NEWSLETTER · 29 Oct 2026, 20:29

Mike Rogers May Have an Imaginary Friend Who Loves Tariffs

View this post on the web at https://www.thebulwark.com/p/mike-rogers-may-have-an-imaginary-friend-who-loves-tariffs

Re-grifter Mike Rogers, the Republican nominee in Michigan’s Senate race, met a guy. A guy with a story. And the guy’s story, which is about getting more work after his company became more successful, has become Rogers’s favorite story. He loves the story so much, he’s been telling it every chance he gets. There are just a few problems: When Rogers tells the story, he often presents it as a recent revelation—a conversation he had just a “couple of weeks ago” or “the other day,” even though he’s told the story at least 31 times over the past year and a half. It’s also not exactly clear who Rogers’s guy is, which industry he works in, or if he is actually a composite guy made up of several people. That’s because Rogers’s anecdote changes rather frequently. The point of Rogers’s story is that Trump’s tariffs have directly made life better for people who work in manufacturing. As Rogers tells it, this guy—who is sometimes several guys or even an entire factory’s worth of guys—has a business that has been able to add a second shift because of increased orders resulting from reshoring, a happy consequence of Trump’s tariffs. The first recorded instance of Rogers relating the anecdote occurred in May 2025, when, during a local radio interview [ https://substack.com/redirect/6032a353-314b-4fee-ba15-63dae00331f1?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ], he said, “I talked to a guy who is putting on a second shift for the first time in twenty-five years because automobile companies are trying to reshore here in the United States. That’s a win, that’s a win for Michigan.” Rogers repeated the story later that week in another interview. (In that instance, he mentioned that the guy worked as a “fabricator.”) Over the rest of the summer, Rogers brought up what the guy told him three more times that we were able to find. This makes sense—it’s a great, illustrative story if your goal is to advocate for and defend Trump’s tariff policy, which Rogers has done throughout his Senate campaign. During a town hall event in September 2025—four months after initially telling the anecdote—Rogers mentioned it again, but this time, he moved up the timestamp to make it a bit more current. “They are starting to repurpose and reshore here in Michigan,” Rogers said. “Talked to a guy just a couple—two weeks ago, adding a second shift. Why? Because the car companies called them and said, ‘Well, I really can’t bring it from China anymore. How about you?’” Rogers would bring up the second-shift man dozens more times over the following year. But as he did, he started tinkering with more details. Check out this supercut of the 31 times we found Rogers telling variations of the story:...

Kate Samuelson

Senior newsletter editor

I wanted to start this newsletter with a huge thank you to the many of you who wrote in to share your memories of living through the 1962 Cuban missile crisis, in response to last week's story https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDQsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABqElIymxNfdiVdHXeAxhuHIF8uAvMvGHfH91Wa93svnu_2jVO5haI7Wc7glS65psAQ0snJ4a_mgb2zAH41WUovYWVhh3ElyXmuN5Vg about the role the Kennedys played in averting nuclear war. It was fascinating reading your recollections - and I've shared a couple of them below. Receiving memories from readers is my favourite thing about writing In History each week - please do keep emailing them in !

I hope you enjoy reading this week's varied edition, which dives into the assassination of South Korea's president in 1979, Princess Margaret's turbulent love life and the enduring mystery of the Loch Ness monster. First up, though, here's a look at how AA Milne's son was affected by being made into a character in a bestselling children's book. See you next week!

VIDEO FROM THE ARCHIVE

'It began to pall when I grew a bit older'

https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDQsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABqElIymyxJShHomA_Pf-7iD9pBU7yUcO9FWv4j88_aXptAx7AeK5vIg6toCkOXS1ke9f82O3mKyzq8yvkp16nQ6D-yL1VEbhj4FYsA Christopher Robin came to resent his father's literary use of the childhood games he played. Credit: Getty Images

AA Milne's first Winnie-the-Pooh book was published 100 years ago, in October 1926, and introduced a cast of characters modelled on the author's son and the soft toys he played with: lovably dim Pooh Bear, nervy Piglet, hyperactive Tigger, pessimistic Eeyore.

From 1966, they became the subject of animated and live-action Disney films, in what is now one of the highest-grossing media franchises of all time.

Yet the reality of Pooh's inception and following success was not easy for Milne's son, who appeared in the books as Christopher Robin. On the BBC's Desert Island Discs in 1976, he said that he was "excited and proud" when the books were first published, but that the thrill of being linked to Winnie-the-Pooh "began to pall when I grew a bit older".

Via the button below, listen to and watch extracts from revealing BBC archive interviews with the man who was desperate to escape from forever being known as Christopher Robin.

FIRST DRAFT OF HISTORY

South Korea's president shot dead

On 26 October 1979, South Korea's President Park Chung-hee was killed by his close friend and security chief. Credit: Alamy

On this week in 1979, South Korea's President Park Chung-hee was shot dead by his long-time friend and the chief of his intelligence service, Kim Jae-gyu. Five other people, including the president's bodyguard, were also killed.

As you can see in the original BBC radio script from 27 October 1979 below, government officials initially said the president was hit "by accident during an argument... at a dinner party in the capital, Seoul". However, the radio report added that "there since have been unconfirmed reports that President Park was assassinated".

An emergency meeting of the cabinet named Prime Minister Choi Kyu-Hua as acting president. Martial law also came into force across the country, "with 600,000 troops on a state of alert, as well as 37,000 American soldiers stationed [in South Korea]", according to the BBC radio script.

WHAT HAPPENED NEXT

A trial and then a retrial

When Park Chung-hee became president in 1961, he had already survived one attempt on his life, which left his wife dead. After his 1979 assassination, he was formally succeeded by acting president Choi Kyu-Hua, who ruled for one year until he was outsted in a military coup.

Kim Jae-gyu, the convicted assassin of President Park Chung-hee, was executed for insurrection in May 1980, along with five others. In the years since, he has remained a deeply polarising figure https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDQsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABqElIym7AL5324XX7dj8FQH1tb4OWyKZLyAn6FBS4yf9II9kQZWi3YxP2Pkoa9RrcfZ2JhPicloRWv8gbcJcyoI96EapaXGNA4GvCw - regarded by some as a killer blinded by power and ambition, and by others as a patriot who sacrificed himself to set South Korea on the path to democracy.

In 2025, almost a half-century after the assassination, Kim Jae-gyu was posthumously retried by the Seoul High Court following years of campaigning by his family and others. The retrial sentencing hearing is set to take place later this year.

Reader memories

Enormous thanks to the many of you who emailed me with your memories of living through the Cuban missile crisis, in response to this story last week https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDQsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABqElIym8ddWu5Y7ppqSMONfySgDUE1j4xK_u0gj5SDlZ-pMICilA9TnES9LF60xRzTsg3d4CeJ6qpmF0pXKq69dGISJjSboWPOnHrg . I was fascinated by your recollections of that terrifying two-week period in 1962, when the world teetered on the brink of nuclear war.

"I was at boarding school in Southern Rhodesia (now Zimbabwe)," wrote reader Hugh Hungerford in Melbourne, Australia. "The school was not even in the capital, but some distance out in the bush. I distinctly remember the senior boys talking about the news, which somehow must have come to us via the teachers' radios. Of course, any nuclear exchange between Cuba and the US would not have directly affected us so many thousands of miles away, but we were nonetheless quite scared. We, along with everyone in the world, breathed a sigh of relief that the confrontation ended peacefully."

"I was a child of 12 years in a small town in western Germany," wrote reader Gabriele Luise Schmitz. "Never before had I experienced an anxiety like that of my father, who had been a soldier in WW2. My fear grew and grew until finally the storm that would have brought disaster had obviously been avoided. It took my parents a long time to get over this crisis and for a long time they praised President Kennedy daily for his leadership and courage."

On this week in 1955, Queen Elizabeth II's sister, Princess Margaret, announced she was ending her engagement https://click.email.bbc.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNDQsInQiOjYzODcsInMiOjZ9AAYAAAAABqElIyop8n3gaAyt7zybp4637Nudi-3Qq3XDE6tcPL7IYEP-hY0X-Da5DAiVI7Os_KyBHwoiMHGxi2eGflIyhdBpOYWRL0JJm5bLJA to Group Captain Peter Townsend, who was divorced with two children. "Mindful of the Church's teaching that Christian marriage is indissoluble... I have resolved to put these considerations before any others," she said in a statement. Five years later, she married the photographer Antony Armstrong-Jones. They divorced in 1978.

NEWSLETTER · 29 Oct 2026, 16:59

Open Tabs: Closing Strong

View this post on the web at https://crookedmedia.substack.com/p/open-tabs-closing-strong

State of Play: The Okey-Doke We started the Tuesday show with a “battle of the closers” segment comparing Donald Trump and Barack Obama’s final messages to voters in the battlegrounds. I regret to inform you that we may have to do something similar today, because Obama was in Georgia last night and Trump was in Ohio, and the split screen was just too good. Even the parts that weren’t about chain restaurants. In Chillicothe, Trump free-associated about asses, Miss Universe, Ilhan Omar — whom he called “a fresh little thing [ https://substack.com/redirect/eaee20b7-dabe-4fe0-9d7b-5524ee82a84b?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ]”— White House renovations, deporting Haitians, why Vivek Ramaswamy’s vile ad against Amy Acton is actually great, and renaming Lake Ontario. Meanwhile, the Atlanta event was classic Obama in the best sense. He spent a lot of it going after Trump and Republicans for getting so offended about his Cheesecake Factory line a few days earlier [ https://substack.com/redirect/a9160ba4-dbaf-49e6-9928-50804b04bb53?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ], in his vintage happy warrior style, stroking his chin and laughing at his own jokes. But what made it such a compelling contrast to his successor was that behind the laughs and the hugs and anecdotes, there was a tight argument [ https://substack.com/redirect/af9fb1c1-48ea-4c0d-b272-260d4c207b36?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] about how Republicans have squandered their chance to try to make your life better: “This is what I mean when I say these are not serious people. They don’t have solutions to actual problems, so they’d rather have the media and you talking about the Cheesecake Factory. That’s the game they’ve been playing for the last two years. Just keep throwing distractions out there — attacks, fabrications, phony outrage — and hope that you won’t notice that your bills are higher and your life is tougher. Except that okey-doke will not work this time, Atlanta.” My only note is that “the okey-doke” is a little too obscure [ https://substack.com/redirect/3d9e3cc3-24b9-4c1f-a291-b3785b2c75d8?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] and silly-sounding to warrant being an Obama catch-phrase all these years, but whatever. Big cheers. I grant: it’s a lot easier to run on throwing the bums out than it is to run on your own accomplishments. If Democrats win back one or both chambers, the messaging two years from now will be, to say the least, more challenging. But one of the things Obama has always been so good at is telling a story about why the world is the way it is — and how we can make it better — in a way that people understand and feel is true. And he’s not the only one. Jon Ossoff, who brought Obama onstage, is great at it, too. So are lots of other Democrats running for office this year, from all parts of the liberal spectrum, from Abdul El-Sayed in Michigan to Paige Cognetti in Pennsylvania to James Talarico in Texas. On the flip side: all you really hear from Republicans right now is division and fear-mongering and excuses and Joe Biden-trashing and general grievance, even though they’ve had two years in full control of the country. In other words, the okey-doke. As Obama says, it doesn’t seem to be working. In a few days, we’ll know for sure. What We’re Watching Somehow we have arrived at the final Friday taping before the midterms. We’ve talked to all the frontline Senate candidates who were able to make time for us, lots of great House candidates, and tremendous gubernatorial candidates, too. (It looks like the clock may run out before we’re able to work out a slot that works for Amy Acton — we’re still trying!) So today, I think we’ll try to zoom out a bit and look at the whole picture. Lay of the land: We had a hunch that we’d wake up to some new Times/Siena results, but it didn’t happen. We’ll have to settle for CNN/SSRS showing Democrats ahead [ https://substack.com/redirect/2867b9c0-bd0c-430f-b068-574c53423583?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] in four key states. We’ll go through the near-final predictions from Nate’s FLIPR model [ https://substack.com/redirect/70b9cbaa-3954-4723-8c7b-899596f2927c?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] — I’m too superstitious to actually print them here — and what we can tell about the race from where Trump and Republicans are spending their time and money, and how the vibes are feeling. The Alaska situation. I haven’t talked about this one with Dan and Jon, but I think it’s safe to say that while it’s probably not fatal that Peltola’s team and the DSCC actively recruited the clone Dan Sullivan, the revelations aren’t exactly helpful. At minimum, it gives Republicans something to go on offense about. You can bet Trump will be talking about it all day, and this morning, Alaska’s term-limited Republican governor said he’s launching an investigation [ https://substack.com/redirect/a925cf77-d92c-43b4-966e-bcff5adf8ff4?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ]. In some good news: Lisa Murkowski just endorsed [ https://substack.com/redirect/cc34f473-051d-4850-8535-e834262fc3b5?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] Jonathan Kreiss-Tomkins, the Democrat running to replace him. South Carolina. Is the Palmetto State truly in play? Trump seems to think so. He’s back in South Carolina this afternoon to rally Republicans around Sen. Darline Graham, who’s facing a surprisingly tough challenge from Democratic nominee Annie Andrews. Trump has reason to be anxious: the latest New York Times/Siena poll [ https://substack.com/redirect/8a8db115-62b0-440f-acf2-577cc2f7ecda?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] shows Graham and Andrews neck-and-neck at 48 percent each, with 3 percent of voters undecided. Trump visited [ https://substack.com/redirect/f7dfc9f0-4281-48ab-b327-c7136fe84192?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] the state just last week. Now he seems to think it needs another boos. Election protection. New reporting from ProPublica [ https://substack.com/redirect/c572dd6b-f53e-4dce-b79b-6d3549db79cc?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] provides yet another spooky look at Trump’s long-running obsession with tilting the election system in his favor. You may remember Cleta Mitchell, the Trump ally and conservative attorney who helped supercharge his attempt to overturn the 2020 results. Apparently, she enjoys an “open door” to the president, and has been “in and out to see him all the time.” She runs an organization dedicated to rewriting election law to make voting more restrictive. Meanwhile, MS NOW reports [ https://substack.com/redirect/1577bd7d-53e1-4277-a5c6-b7700b08c94e?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] that FBI agents worry they may be sent to key swing states to patrol polling stations or even seize voting machines. Last night, the Department of Justice insisted before a judge that FBI and ICE agents won’t serve [ https://substack.com/redirect/67de06e9-2598-4453-ab50-9f0b166d68dc?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] as poll monitors, although personnel from other agencies, like DEA and ATF, will. Here’s hoping all this comes to nothing. Open Tabs For this final week of the midterms: tea-leaf reading on allllll the Senate races, why Roger Marshall’s neighbors don’t like him, and — why not! — an exploration of a Substack founder’s claim that this platform has righted the web’s wrongs...

Unsubscribe https://substack.com/redirect/2/eyJlIjoiaHR0cHM6Ly9jcm9va2VkbWVkaWEuc3Vic3RhY2suY29tL2FjdGlvbi9kaXNhYmxlX2VtYWlsP3Rva2VuPWV5SjFjMlZ5WDJsa0lqbzBNVGczTVRjM05qZ3NJbkJ2YzNSZmFXUWlPakl5TWpZMU9EUXdPU3dpYzJWamRHbHZibDlwWkNJNk16QTFPRE00TENKcFlYUWlPakUzT1RNeU9EazNPRElzSW1WNGNDSTZNVGd5TkRneU5UYzRNaXdpYVhOeklqb2ljSFZpTFRVeE1EWTFOakFpTENKemRXSWlPaUprYVhOaFlteGxYMlZ0WVdsc0luMC5ick5NWXdNNndBeTlDa1VSbklQOTFkcG9wUkl6THl5azY5WDRLQV9qOGZNJnNlY3Rpb25faWQ9MzA1ODM4IiwicCI6MjIyNjU4NDA5LCJzIjo1MTA2NTYwLCJmIjp0cnVlLCJ1Ijo0MTg3MTc3NjgsImlhdCI6MTc5MzI4OTc4MiwiZXhwIjoyMTA4ODY1NzgyLCJpc3MiOiJwdWItMCIsInN1YiI6ImxpbmstcmVkaXJlY3QifQ.f-PoIk_pROMvaWjeaQKvPxQTdm_9-N5Pw5KMH3WQiqU?

NEWSLETTER · 29 Oct 2026, 16:56

The Why Axis. BRAZIL. DEFENCE. HEAT PUMPS

29/10/2026 | www.bruegel.org (https://www.bruegel.org/) View newsletter in your browser (https://mailchi.mp/bruegel/the-why-axis-ets-common-eu-debt-bam-10634885?e=6912435cb1) Was this email forwarded to you? Subscribe (https://bruegel.my.salesforce-sites.com/events/UpdateAcc) to receive Bruegel’s updates directly in your inbox.

Europe’s spending on defence equipment is set to more than double (https://www.nato.int/content/dam/nato/webready/documents/finance/def-exp-2026-en.pdf) between 2022 and 2026, but its procurement market remains fragmented. Recent data shows that only 24% (https://eda.europa.eu/docs/default-source/eda-publications/2026-eda_defencedata_webpublic.pdf) of European Union defence equipment procurement was collaborative, well below the 35% benchmark. National preferences for bespoke models keep orders small and competition weak, and give firms little reason to expand production.

Several initiatives tackle different parts of this problem: the EU’s SAFE programme offers cheaper loans for joint purchases; NATO works to match demand with industrial capacity; and the proposed Multilateral Defence Mechanism (MDM) and Defence, Security and Resilience Bank (DSRB) would add more multilateral finance. No initiative yet combines large pooled orders with a binding commitment to open contracts to partners’ firms, meaning that more spending is unlikely to strengthen competition.

An expanded MDM could address this gap. Starting with countries whose military requirements already overlap, the MDM could turn agreement on individual capabilities into a funded, multi-year procurement programme. Governments would agree common requirements first, then let suppliers from all participating countries compete. This would offer multiple benefits: countries with high borrowing costs would get access to cheaper finance, while others could be attracted by shared stocks, a larger supplier pool and faster delivery. Even big producers with little need to borrow, like Germany, would have good reason to join to ensure their firms have access to an expanding market as pooled orders grow.

We recommend starting with standardised capabilities such as 155mm ammunition, testing whether pooled demand changes industrial investment decisions and using existing NATO and European procurement agencies to manage programmes. Ideally, the MDM and DSRB would merge, keeping separate arms for procurement and industrial finance.

This could provide a practical route from protected national procurement towards a larger, more competitive European defence market. Read the Policy Brief, 'From fragmented procurement to a European defence market (https://www.bruegel.org/policy-brief/fragmented-procurement-european-defence-market) ', by Gene Frieda Do you have questions for our experts? Send them to press@bruegel.org.

Bill Kristol & Andrew Egger - The Bulwark

1 item in this evidence packet.

NEWSLETTER · 29 Oct 2026, 14:28

Is This Heaven? No, It’s the Midterms.

View this post on the web at https://www.thebulwark.com/p/is-this-heaven-no-its-the-midterms-trump-democrats-midterms-brewers-hegseth-general-fired-pentagon-poll

“Any election that doesn’t go my way is fraud.” That’s the Trump model, and his right-wing allies around the world are starting to embrace it too. “The elections in the state of Israel were stolen by Arab parties that support terrorism and the elimination of the Jewish state,” Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu posted yesterday on X [ https://substack.com/redirect/4fc1475c-884d-4083-a93b-af1acdce25bd?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ]. “We demand from the police and the Shin Bet a thorough investigation into the election theft by the Arab parties and a full prosecution of the criminals.” Law enforcement, he said, “must act with an iron fist and in an immediate manner.” Not every such claim gets Trump’s blessing, however. Brazil’s Flávio Bolsonaro, who fled the country after his own election loss this week, yesterday posted a letter thanking Trump and Argentinian president Javier Milei for helping him escape to the United States. In it, he declared himself the “true president-elect of Brazil.” But Trump immediately poured water on the claim: “I’m not going to make a comment about that,” he told reporters. “I won’t get involved.” Bolsonaro subsequently deleted the letter and reposted it, sans president-elect claim. Happy Thursday. Baseball, Politics, and War by William Kristol A quick look this morning at three pieces of news from yesterday evening: one about sports, one about politics, and one about war. The Milwaukee Brewers are the World Series champions. It was the first World Series victory for the franchise, which came into existence as an expansion team in 1969. A mere 57 years later (!), they took it all. They pulled this off while playing in the league’s smallest media market, and with baseball’s nineteenth-biggest payroll. And they did it impressively and decisively, defeating the Padres 3–1 in the division championship series, taking the league championship 4–0 over the defending champion Los Angeles Dodgers, and then putting away Tampa Bay in the World Series 4 games to 1. The Brewers’ 11–2 postseason record this year was one of the four best performances in the last thirty years. One might even call what happened this year a Brew Wave. (Sorry.) The Brewers’ talent and momentum overcame the obstacles of money and history. Perhaps a good omen for next week? Don’t let the midterms pass without signing up for the best reporting, analysis, and commentary in defense of democracy. Join us. Speaking of omens, here’s another one. A couple of hours before the first pitch last night, Donald Trump posted an announcement to Truth Social: I’m going to be doing a major TeleRally for Julia Letlow, who is running for the U.S. Senate and is spectacular, and our fantastic Speaker of the House, Mike Johnson, on Monday evening, LIVE. The state where Julia Letlow is running for the Senate is Louisiana—which Trump won by a 22 percent margin just two years ago. This is a race where both national Democrats and Republicans have spent almost no money, assuming it couldn’t be competitive. But a poll [ https://substack.com/redirect/5faf8bb4-1679-4b61-9a10-3919ad3407ea?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] a month ago (admittedly sponsored by the campaign of the Democratic nominee, Jamie Davis, but done by a very reputable pollster, Hart Research) showed a 4-point race. And since then we’ve seen an early vote in Louisiana that is even more bullish for Davis. (Lauren Egan reported yesterday [ https://substack.com/redirect/fb91c060-0d11-4b64-9d15-d56500dc6f14?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] on how encouraging the high early voting numbers in predominantly black areas are for Democrats.) Davis is a talented and hard-working—though underfunded—candidate. Kind of like the Brewers. Letlow, by contrast, has been mailing it in, assuming that in the general election as in the primary, her “complete and total endorsement” by Donald Trump would carry her to victory. But the Hart poll showed Trump’s approval underwater in the state. And with Democrats now, I believe, on a path to victory in neighboring Texas, and with neighboring Mississippi on the other side clearly in play (Trump visited there Monday), the Louisiana alarm bells seem to have belatedly gone off. Thus the last-minute “TeleRally” on Monday. But could it be like Tampa Bay’s rally to win the fourth game Sunday night and bring the Series to 3–1: too little, too late? Just a word about the state of the race nationally: More than 40 million Americans have voted early, about one third of this year’s likely total turnout. Early voting is tricky to interpret, but I’d say that in general the early voting is even more bullish for Democrats than the polls. The polls show a non-trivial number of voters persuaded this year to switch to the Democrats. The early vote suggests a higher turnout among Democrats. This combination of persuasion and turnout is like having very good pitching and timely hitting. Throw in a little momentum, and you really can be off to the races. Which, for whatever it’s worth, I think the Democrats are. The baseball season is over. The election season will soon be over. But the real world goes on. Which leads me to the third news item from yesterday, an item that provides a reminder of what’s at stake. As reported late yesterday [ https://substack.com/redirect/c2bcbd46-ede6-4d19-ab6e-14da8c850333?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] in the Atlantic and then confirmed by the Pentagon, Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth has fired another general officer. Yesterday’s victim of Hegseth’s purge of our professional and nonpartisan military was the Pentagon’s acting chief planner, Lt. Gen. Brett Sylvia. He was ousted from the important job of director of strategy, plans, and policy at the Joint Chiefs of Staff, the position known inside the military as the J5—a position which he had taken after its previous incumbent was also forced out by Hegseth. We don’t know why Sylvia was dismissed after thirty-two years of distinguished service. Perhaps it was because he’d been attacked on social media last year by the Trump administration’s own Madame Defarge, Laura Loomer. Perhaps he had doubts about the wisdom of relaunching the Iran war. Or perhaps he had resisted Hegseth’s dumbing down or politicization of the military in some way. In any case, Silva is the fifth Army general who’s been ousted this year, including very well-respected figures like Chief of Staff Gen. Randy George and Gen. Chris “Donahue, commander of Army forces in Europe and Africa. At least twenty-six admirals and generals have stepped down or been pushed out since Hegseth took control of the Pentagon less than two years ago. Of course, this comes as rumors swirl—and Trump himself speculates—about a relaunching of the war on Iran soon after the elections, and amid reports that a third U.S. aircraft carrier and thousands of additional troops are making their way to the region. Rep. Letlow has voted for the war and supported it, as have virtually all of her Republican colleagues in Congress. Jamie Davis, like almost all Democrats running this year, has said he’ll vote to stop Trump and Hegseth’s reckless and foolish war. Baseball is fun. Elections are important. War is serious. Perhaps Hegseth’s latest purge will provide an additional reminder of why Americans should turn out to vote—to put into office men and women who will try to check rather than rubber-stamp the actions of the reckless and foolish administration we’re saddled with for another two years. QUICK HITS NO BAD IDEAS IN A BRAINSTORM: What’s the ideal number of U.S. attorneys at the Justice Department who publicly advance eugenicist views? Less than one, right? Andrew reported yesterday on Ellis Boyle, U.S. Attorney for the Eastern District of North Carolina, who went on a podcast last week and shared some interesting views on crime: Five percent of the population cause 90 percent of the crime. . . . If you took out 5 percent of the population that’s bad and removed them from society, so they don’t create mayhem and create, you know, babies with the gene pool of more bad, then I think, in two, maybe three generations, think of how great this population would be. But that’s something that takes willpower. Read the whole thing. [ https://substack.com/redirect/6c8109e8-d553-43c4-897d-e7cc9518dbf8?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] BRACING FOR IMPACT: For the last two years, big tech companies have judged it wise to snuggle up close to Donald Trump. But with Democrats seemingly on the verge of surging back to power in Washington and eager to scrutinize the corruption around the White House, many are suddenly realizing they’ve put themselves uncomfortably close to the blast zone. Politico reports [ https://substack.com/redirect/a1ce8ad1-d018-4e31-8f3b-10c2ba496746?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ]: A broad range of technology, financial, cryptocurrency and industrial companies are now scrambling to get ahead of Democrats’ aggressive congressional oversight agenda if the party wins power next week, according to nine lobbyists, lawyers, public relations professionals and former congressional staffers who spoke to Politico. They’re taking on outside legal counsel and contacting PR firms, particularly companies that donated to projects including the White House ballroom and Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy’s road trip video series. Law and lobbying firms on K Street have gone on an oversight-focused hiring spree in recent months, adding staff to handle increasing demand from clients. Some firms are reviewing their written communications with the White House to prepare for potential legal or reputational risks. Others are hiring lawyers who specialize in congressional investigations, arranging crisis communications plans or rehearsing for hearings. You sure do hate to see that. Read the whole thing. [ https://substack.com/redirect/a1ce8ad1-d018-4e31-8f3b-10c2ba496746?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] SULLIVAN TALENT SEARCH: There are two Dans Sullivan on the ballot in Alaska this year: the incumbent GOP senator, and a gadfly candidate who’s plainly only there to try to peel away some votes from the incumbent GOP senator. Sullivan’s opponent, Democrat Mary Peltola, has long insisted her campaign had nothing to do with recruiting the Other Dan. But yesterday, the New York Times reported [ https://substack.com/redirect/4a1285cd-0bcf-4e70-bfdb-906a92751634?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] that this wasn’t true, and that Peltola’s campaign had actually gone on what might be described as an Alaska talent search to turf up a Dan Sullivan who could be convinced to run. If the story—which Peltola still denies, but which is supported by lots of documentary evidence obtained by the Times—is true, it’s a very bad look. Both parties have run versions of this dirty play, called “ballot stacking,” in the past. But that doesn’t change the fact that it’s a naked attempt to confuse voters, or the fact that Peltola has insisted all along that her campaign didn’t participate in recruiting the Other Dan. Alaska Gov. Mike Dunleavy, a Republican, announced [ https://substack.com/redirect/741e312a-7a68-4af2-bdd5-48bb490076ea?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] yesterday that he was ordering an investigation into the Peltola campaign “to investigate whether this scheme violated Alaska’s laws.” And unsurprisingly, Trump is already using the story to burnish his own plans to try to meddle with the outcome in Alaska. In a barrage of Truth Social posts yesterday, he (absurdly) demanded that Peltola be removed from the ballot, calling the scheme “Voter Fraud at the Highest Level.” (It wasn’t, and as Trump well knows, even people accused of crimes are allowed to run for office.) “Mary Peltola,” Trump added [ https://substack.com/redirect/a39508d5-5826-4ee5-9497-4cc94ae4919a?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ], “should be removed from the Ballot and prosecuted—So should the FAKE Dan Sullivan. Nothing else will do!” CHEAP SHOTS

NEWSLETTER · 29 Oct 2026, 12:55

Brazil’s Lula Defeats the Far Right: A Victory for Sovereignty and Multipolarity

View this post on the web at https://josricardomartins.substack.com/p/brazils-lula-defeats-the-far-right

Ricardo Martins, [ https://substack.com/redirect/9f86492d-1ff6-4b06-8e55-625781be404b?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] October 28, 2026 Lula’s re-election is more than a domestic political triumph. It’s a direct challenge to the notion that the far right’s advance in Latin America is irreversible, and it reinforces Brazil’s position as a country with an independent foreign policy in a highly contested international order. For the Global South, Europe and Washington, the implications go way beyond Latin America. A Victory with Global Consequences The presidential run-off in Brazil on 25 October had repercussions that extended well beyond the borders of the country. Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva (PT) defeated [ https://substack.com/redirect/0876f1d2-c936-4948-8506-c5313d01bbb7?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] Flávio Bolsonaro (PL) in the run-off with 51.8% of the valid votes to 48.2%. Lula made 64.26 million votes, a margin of 4.43 million. The result was more than just a personal victory for Lula. It was a defeat for the Bolsonaro-aligned movement with Trumpism and a significant setback for the international far right, which has been so keen to harness nationalism, cultural polarisation and the erosion of trust in democratic institutions as political tools. Brazil’s real legacy will be determined by its ability to combine sovereignty with international co-operation, economic development with social justice and democratic institutions with real improvements in the lives of the people The outcome also complicates Donald Trump’s desire to build a right and far-right bloc across the Americas. Flávio Bolsonaro was very much a product of the Trump movement, whereas Lula was advocating for Brazil to have the freedom to pursue its own international, BRICS and Global South course. Lula was very clear on the point: Brazil would not be a subordinate player in Trump and Marco Rubio’s geopolitical project – “We are nobody’s backyard.” His victory shows that the right-wing shift in Latin America is not a universal phenomenon and is by no means irreversible. European leaders were very pleased with the outcome. Von der Leyen [ https://substack.com/redirect/bdb5614a-2e72-4495-bda5-949b6f29ff6d?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] went so far as to say that democracy and multilateralism had won in Brazil. That’s a reflection of a wider concern and shows that Trump’s popularity in Europe is low. As international co-operation is increasingly under threat from the rise of nationalist governments and the intensification of great power competition, Brazil is still an important partner on climate, trade and multilateralism. BRICS has gained with Lula victory. Popular Mobilisation and the Digital Battleground Lula’s victory was not just the result of the established party machinery. Had the party left by itself, it would have lost the election. The second round saw a massive resurgence of youth mobilisation with students, artists, activists and all sorts of progressive social groups getting out in force as the prospect of a Flávio Bolsonaro presidency became real. Social media was absolutely key to Lula’s victory too. Federal Deputy André Janones was instrumental in getting the message out via the ‘Exército de Lula’ WhatsApp* group (which Agência Brasil reported [ https://substack.com/redirect/77d6ce67-92ea-4870-896d-fb5816f64115?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] had 10.8 million members). The campaign became much more direct and informal, getting the message out to people in ways that went well beyond the normal channels of political communication. This was clearly a mobilisation driven by a wider fear that a new Bolsonaro government would attack the democratic safety net, erode social protection and deepen the fractures around religion, identity and national belonging. For many people, the election was about defending democracy and the social gains of public health, public education, workers’ rights and environmental protection. The digital world also presented a major challenge for democracies everywhere. There were serious allegations of political bias and differential treatment by the social media platforms, particularly Meta*. Folha de S.Paulo [ https://substack.com/redirect/e98ce16a-de98-4f3d-881f-174447175996?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] reporting on internal Meta messages described the restriction of Lula-related accounts, and Meta* attributed the incidents to technical problems, denying any preference for a candidate. However, the “technical problems” and curbing of message boosts occurred only in Lula’s campaign. Nevertheless, the issue is international. If private platforms determine political visibility through opaque algorithms, democratic accountability cannot be confined to the ballot box. In his victory speech, Lula promised to further regulate digital platforms in Brazil, entering a broader international debate over technological sovereignty and the extent of corporate power and influence over politics. Social Justice as a Foundation of Democracy Brazil’s election was also about the future of the state and its duty to reduce inequality. Programmes such as Bolsa Família (the income transfer programme for the poor), minimum wage increases, free public health and free education aren’t just domestic policy. They are a political statement about whether society at large should share the benefits of economic development or whether they should be concentrated in the hands of a few. The World Bank [ https://substack.com/redirect/a4140f96-e924-4d62-a294-688caec5fa4f?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] has connected Brazil’s recent poverty reduction with the rise in minimum wages, the expansion of Bolsa Família and better labour market conditions. The INPE report [ https://substack.com/redirect/90834297-fd43-44d4-ba25-58673982ccd8?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] for the 2025-26 monitoring period also shows significant reductions in deforestation in both the Amazon and the Cerrado — 41.8%. A Bolsonaro government would burn these environmental gains. Further, according to the renowned American political scientist Robert Dahl [ https://substack.com/redirect/4cd90d14-c511-4cda-9498-1b038494bf53?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ], democracy is not only holding elections. Democracy is undermined when you have elections which change governments but do not address the fundamental issues of economic insecurity, social exclusion and preserving the rights of minorities. The far right in Latin America needs to understand that democratic institutions can actually improve people’s lives. Lula’s mandate will therefore be to preserve existing programmes while delivering employment, public security, economic stability, and better services. He is up against a conservative Congress and significant fiscal constraints. His opponent’s 48.2% share of the vote is a clear indication of a deeply divided nation. Brazil, BRICS and Strategic Autonomy The geopolitical stakes are very high given that Brazil is a major economy, a major agricultural and natural resources power, including oil and rare earths, and a significant voice in the Global South. Its foreign policy has to keep in mind the balance of power among Washington, Beijing, Brussels, and the rest of the BRICS. Lula has consistently maintained that Brazil has the right to maintain relations with the US, China, Russia, the EU, India, and all the other partners it sees fit without having to take any external direction demanded by the U.S. Lula’s core commitment is to BRICS, to South-South cooperation and to a more multipolar world order. Strategic autonomy doesn’t necessarily mean hostility toward Washington. Brazil can negotiate with the US while deepening trade with China, Russia and India, expanding relationships with emerging economies, and standing up for its interests in international organisations. This is of course all the more important as the competition intensifies around critical minerals, energy, advanced technology and supply chains. In his victory speech, Lula reaffirmed that Brazil’s natural resources should underpin domestic industrial development, scientific research, and better-paid jobs, rather than simply exporting raw materials and importing finished goods. The same principle underpins Brazil’s multilateral diplomacy. Through BRICS and the various other fora, Brasilia can be a useful bridge between countries that don’t necessarily have the same interests. Its regional leadership has been eroded by the rise of the far-right in Latin America, but Lula’s election gives Brazil another chance to re-establish a degree of co-operation without necessarily requiring ideological alignment. It is a good sign that Latin American leaders have congratulated Lula on his victory, except for Argentina’s Milei. Europe also has an interest in the multi-alignment approach. Cooperation with Brazil on trade, climate, and the EU-Mercosur agreement should be built on mutual interest and respect for sovereignty, not on the expectation that Brazil must choose between Washington and Beijing. The Victory Is Only the Beginning Lula’s victory by no means ends the far right. The strength of the right and far-right bloc in Congress will constrain his agenda, and the government will face serious tests in the areas of public security, living costs, employment and the fiscal position. Flávio Bolsonaro’s refusal to accept the result and his clear intention to contest it all point to the continued stress on the political institutions. However, his contestation point is weak. He argues that the rules were changed during the election process because the voting time was extended by two hours. The Workers’ Party requested it because of a lack of public transportation in some major cities — governed by Flávio’s allies, such as São Paulo and Salvador — that did not comply with the electoral court rules to provide a weekday and not a weekend bus schedule. The Workers’ Party electorate depends heavily on public transportation. Bolsonaro’s own party also requested the extension of the voting time because of heavy rain and floods in southern Brazil, where he gains more votes. So, his complaint is void. Lula has to govern beyond his own electorate. He has to negotiate without compromising his principles and has to turn the popular mobilisation that helped to win into genuine participation in public life. The young people who were so instrumental in the run-off cannot be simply treated as a vote. Their involvement is a statement that they want to play a real role in shaping the future of Brazil. In his victory speech, Lula promised greater youth and public participation in this government’s decision-making. For the international community, the message is just as important. The far right is not invincible, and the emergence of a multipolar world doesn’t mean that every country has to go down the same ideological path. Brazil has reasserted its democratic choice and its right to act independently on the world stage, not just bow to the Monroe Doctrine, rebranded by Trump as Donroe. But electoral victory is only the first step. Brazil’s real legacy will be determined by its ability to combine sovereignty with international co-operation, economic development with social justice and democratic institutions with real improvements in the lives of the people. That is the true test of Lula’s fourth term and the lesson his victory will offer far beyond Brazil itself.

Financing Europe's defence build-up: National budgets and beyond

Financing Europe's defence build-up: National budgets and beyond. Financing Europe's defence build-up: National budgets and beyond christian.diet… Thu, 10/29/2026 - 10:45 18 minutes. Defence budgets across Europe are rising. Russia’s war of aggression against Ukraine, combined with doubts over the future of US security guarantees, has prompted European governments to invest more in defence. According to EUISS calculations based on EDA and NATO data, spending by EU Member States was 56% higher in 2025 than it was in 2022, after accounting for inflation. One challenge is sustaining and increasing this spending Rebuilding Europe’s military capacity will require investment over many years. Yet many national budgets are under pressure from fiscal constraints, higher borrowing costs and competing domestic priorities. Europeans also need to spend more cooperatively to reduce costs and secure economies of scale. The feasibility of cooperation is influenced by many factors, including national threat perceptions, operational cultures, defence planning cycles and national industrial interests, but well-designed financial incentives can make cooperation more attractive. A second challenge is mobilising the investment needed for Europe’s broader defence ramp-up , including expanding defence production capacity, investing in R&D and making European infrastructure more resilient to possible sabotage or attack. Europe’s defence financing instruments can all play a role in meeting these challenges. This landscape has grown increasingly complex. National budgets remain the main pillar of defence expenditure, but they now form part of a wider ecosystem of EU instruments, public financial institutions like the European Investment Bank (EIB) and private capital, whose role continues to expand. This analysis maps the landscape of European defence financing and assesses the growing number of proposals to strengthen and expand this ecosystem, such as a potential new round of funding under the EU’s Security Action for Europe (SAFE) instrument or multilateral borrowing mechanisms outside the EU framework. Ultimately, there is no silver bullet for Europe’s defence financing challenge. There is no alternative to sustained national spending, but it can and should be supplemented by additional sources of funding. While this will not remove the need for difficult fiscal choices, it will make higher defence spending easier and help drive Europe’s broader defence ramp-up. The core: National spending. EU Member States have significantly increased defence expenditure since Russia’s annexation of Crimea in 2014. In nominal terms, spending rose by approximately €89 billion between 2014 and 2022, reaching €239.75 billion. Since then, spending has risen to €417.62 billion in 2025, an increase of 74%. After adjusting for inflation, this accounts for an increase of 56% between 2022 and 2025. The current average level of spending.
